Billerud · BILL-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 19 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Billerud

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 47,352 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “57th percentile” means the stock beats 57% of them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

D5442nd percentile

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces have turned from strongly adverse (2023 to 2025: wood doubling, destocking, European oversupply, krona strength) to moderately supportive for about the next 6 to 12 months. The support comes from the spread, not from demand. Fresh-fibre scarcity lifts the prices of Billerud's most important grades, lagged Swedish wood relief lowers its largest cost, and biomass energy insulates it from the gas shock that is hurting recycled rivals. That is enough to take EBITDA from about SEK 3.4bn (2026) to about SEK 4.5bn to 4.7bn (2027) without any demand recovery. Beyond twelve months the forces turn into balancing loops, and each has a date:

  • salvage felling ends and Swedish wood rises, on management's own implied view of a flat 2027 average;
  • UK recycled capacity from March 2027 narrows the kraftliner premium;
  • hedges roll off by Q2 2027 into a stronger consensus krona;
  • carbon costs reappear under ETS2 in 2028.

The Consumer should therefore model 2027 as a year when price gains are increasingly offset by cost and currency, rather than one when the 2026 relief compounds. External change is positive for the near-term investment case and negative for the mid-term one.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0253C-0560C-0735C-0950C-3544C-3935
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

B+5389th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is diverging by grade. It is easing in about 60% of Billerud's sales (virgin kraftliner, sack, semichemical fluting, tariff-protected North America) and intensifying in the rest (cartonboard, LPB exports to Asia, and from March 2027 the recycled-versus-virgin price gap). The net direction for the next six months is slightly favourable; for 6 to 18 months it is neutral to adverse.

  • Easing. No new fresh-fibre kraftliner machine is planned in Europe. North American producers closed nearly 4 million short tons (10% of capacity) in 2025 to mid-2026. Unbleached kraftliner rose EUR 200 a tonne from its Q1 2026 low, and its premium over recycled testliner reached EUR 230 a tonne. Sack prices rose EUR 50 a tonne into Q4 2026, under Mondi's price leadership. The October 2026 gas spike raises recycled rivals' costs but not Billerud's.
  • Intensifying. Stora Enso's 750kt Oulu line is ramping to full capacity by the end of 2027, and its cumulative start-up losses of EUR 190m give it a strong motive to fill the machine. Chinese virgin cartonboard exports doubled to 3.8Mt in 2025. Western Europe added a net 860kt of capacity in 2025. Asian LPB competition is growing. In North America, Sappi's Somerset SBS conversion and Graphic Packaging's SBS put pressure on the board grades Billerud is entering.
  • The dated turn. About 1.45Mt of recycled containerboard (Palm King's Lynn 700kt from March 2027; Eren Shotton 750kt in 2027) is the first dated event that could narrow the virgin premium. Morgan Stanley says 1.72Mt of recycled ramp-ups "could easily keep the fresh-fibre market in check".

Over the last 12 to 18 months intensity was worse (European overcapacity and wood inflation together). It is better now in the grades that matter most to Billerud's margin, but this is on a 12 to 24 month clock.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

A6093rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: input costs are a clear tailwind in 2026, worth roughly SEK 0.9bn to 1.0bn of gross wood relief, partly offset by SEK 0.2bn to 0.4bn of chemical, logistics and energy inflation. For 2027 they are neutral at best, with a downside skew. The relief came from a temporary supply surplus (storm-felled timber and mill downtime), not from a structural change, so it is the most reversible tailwind in the case.

  • Wood (35% of operating costs, up from 32% in 2023). Nordic pulpwood fell 20% to 30% from its mid-2025 peak, and Swedish prices fell 15% to 20% from Q3 2025 highs. Storm Johannes felled 10 million cubic metres. The P&L lags spot by three to six months, so the benefit is concentrated in H2 2026 and Q1 2027. Management guides Q4 2026 wood flat and 2027 average wood costs stable against 2026 "despite potential pressure after storm felling ceases".
  • The insight in that guidance. The Q4 2026 exit rate sits below the 2026 average, because H1 2026 still carried lagged peak costs. A flat 2027 average therefore implies that management expects spot wood prices to rise during 2027. There is no further wood tailwind in management's own frame. Upside exists only if spot prices stay at today's levels; then 2027 would gain perhaps SEK 0.4bn to 0.7bn.
  • The structural deficit. About 10% of Nordic supply is gone because of the Russian ban, Finnish prices rebounded 10% to 15% from Q1 lows, and DNB Carnegie warns that Nordic wood "will surge again in 2027 as demand recovers".
  • Supplier concentration. Swedish forest-owner associations and the pricelist set about two-thirds of European wood purchases. Mitigants: the Sveaskog agreement (2024), more than 15% direct purchases from private owners, the Bergvik Skog Öst felling-rights agreement to 2034, and the Trätåg rail stake.
  • Chemicals and logistics. Up on Middle East shipping disruption. They offset most of the Q2 wood relief, renegotiated chemical contracts gave SEK 130m of relief in Q1, and the Q4 2026 input-cost headwind is about SEK 60m, two-thirds of it logistics.
  • Energy. A relative advantage: about 98% fossil-free in Europe and biomass-based, so the October 2026 gas spike (EUR 78/MWh) hurts recycled rivals, not Billerud. North America is 28% fossil-based.
  • Relative position. Central European wood is rising (Czech +14% to 15%; Mondi has a EUR 35m half-on-half headwind), so Billerud's cost position against Mondi improved. Against SCA and Holmen, which own forests, it improves only while wood is falling.

Net: a tailwind now, fading to neutral in 2027, with a reversal risk that peaks in H2 2027 when salvage felling ends.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F3523rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro forces bite hardest at the company level, through currency, because Billerud's cost-and-revenue mismatch is an outlier in its sector. European consumer weakness bites at the sector level, and interest rates bite at the industry level (construction, and therefore sack paper).

  • Company level: the krona. Only 18% of revenue is in SEK, but over 55% of costs are. The EUR exposure is 90% transactional. A 10% stronger krona removes about SEK 640m of EBITDA before hedges (EUR SEK 300m, USD SEK 260m, GBP SEK 80m). That is about 18% of 2026 consensus EBITDA and about 13% of 2027 consensus. The consensus FX path is EUR/SEK from 11.00 to 10.50 within twelve months (about 4.5% krona strength), with dispersion to 10.40. DNB Carnegie says 10.40 would neutralise the expected SEK 900m of wood relief.
  • Hedging only delays the effect. EUR flows are 80% hedged in Q1 2027 but 38% in Q2 2027, and USD flows are 35% hedged by Q2 2027. Hedge rates (EUR 10.97, USD 9.33) are close to spot, so they delay rather than offset any move. In 2025 a stronger krona removed SEK 1,607m of revenue through translation.
  • Sector level. Weak European consumption (German GfK -30.8, euro area -17.9) and down-trading suppress cartonboard and luxury board. North America is solid.
  • Industry level. High rates depress construction (sack paper) and, through the discount rate, materials multiples. The FfF regime is risk-off beneath the index: Fragility at Double Underweight, Momentum at Underweight, Materials, the Sector and Sweden at Neutral. Middle East disruption raises freight and chemical costs and is a single trigger with oil.

Net: macro is a headwind, with currency the largest single item and the one most under-modelled by the sell-side (Jefferies and DNB Carnegie both cut estimates on FX in 2025 and 2026).

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–50

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three other dynamics matter. One is positive optionality (European consolidation), one is a valuation cross-current (forest asset listings), and one is a negative tail (North American SBS oversupply and tariff reliance).

  1. European consolidation pressure (positive optionality). Jefferies argues Europe needs more than 5% of permanent closures, and up to 400kt in Billerud's grades. Weaker players are exiting: Smurfit Westrock's Birmingham mill, the Wizpaper liquidation, Metsä Board's Tako closure and Stora Enso's Skutskär line. Billerud's chief executive says closures and M&A are "on the table". Any industry closure in cartonboard or kraft would raise operating rates. That is the one external change that could make the SEK 4.3bn to 4.8bn European target plausible.
  2. Forest asset listings (Stora Enso's Bergslagets Skogar: EGM on 15 December 2026, listing by H1 2027). These put a public price on Nordic forest, which sharpens the market's view of who owns wood and who does not. Billerud owns none, so the read-across is a reminder of its structural cost disadvantage rather than a valuation support. Billerud's own "NAV" discount (45%, Jefferies, April 2026) rests on mill replacement value, which is a weaker anchor.
  3. North America (negative tail). US SBS is oversupplied (Clearwater, Pacific Southwest Container). Sappi's Somerset conversion adds capacity, and one US expert calls Billerud a "wild card". If tariffs were removed or exporters absorbed the 15% duty, the domestic advantage would shrink.
  4. The energy cross-current. The October 2026 gas spike is a relative tailwind for biomass-fuelled Swedish mills. If gas falls below EUR 40/MWh, recycled containerboard prices fall (Jefferies), which narrows the virgin premium.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D4426th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the threat of new entry is real but indirect for Billerud's core grades.

  • Adjacent entrants, the largest threat. Graphic-paper machines converted to recycled containerboard: Palm King's Lynn 700kt (March 2027), Eren Shotton 750kt (2027), Norske Skog Golbey and Heinzel Laakirchen already. They do not make virgin kraftliner, but they lower testliner prices and so narrow the virgin premium from EUR 230 a tonne. This is a medium-to-high threat from mid-2027.
  • Standard entrants. Chinese and Asian virgin cartonboard and LPB producers, with backward pulp integration and state-aligned financing. A high threat in Asian export markets, and medium in Europe as quality gaps narrow. US SBS capacity (Sappi Somerset, Graphic Packaging) threatens Billerud's new North American board. Barriers stay high in European LPB: qualification takes years and greenfield integrated mills cost more than EUR 1.5bn.
  • Orthogonal or innovative entrants. Barrier-coating innovators, moulded fibre (Metsä's Muoto), and Asian LPB entrants (Greatview in converting). A low threat within 18 months.

For the model: build in a narrowing of the kraftliner and sack premium from H2 2027, rather than a loss of volume.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F3522nd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are a temporary support and a structural risk. Nordic forest owners and their associations (Södra, Mellanskog, Norra Skog), Sveaskog and the felling-rights counterparty BSÖ (Bergvik Skog Öst; SEK 551m of related-party procurement in 2025) gained pricing power after the 2022 Russian ban. Pulpwood doubled between 2021 and 2025. In 2026 that power has been weakened by three things:

  • storm-felled timber (Storm Johannes, 10 million cubic metres);
  • mill downtime across the region (UPM Kaukas, Metsä Joutseno);
  • more supply from private owners after price signals.

So Swedish prices fell 15% to 20% from Q3 2025, and forest-owner associations cut price lists. Management's guidance of a flat 2027 average, with "potential pressure after storm felling ceases", acknowledges that this support will fade. Other suppliers:

  • chemical suppliers renegotiated (SEK 130m of relief in Q1 2026) and then raised prices on Middle East costs;
  • logistics suppliers are inflating (two-thirds of the Q4 SEK 60m headwind);
  • energy comes from the Bomhus Energi joint arrangement (SEK 352m in 2025), plus own biomass and hedged power.

For the model: treat wood as a 2026 support that turns into a 2027 to 2028 risk, worth roughly SEK 0.9bn per 10% move. It is the largest single risk in the supply chain.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0138C-0343C-0434C-0863C-3245C-3737C-4335
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F3813th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: end-market demand is cyclically bottoming, not recovering. It is structurally mixed, and only one part of the mix (liquid packaging board) has visibility longer than two quarters. None of Billerud's markets is in a cyclical upswing with two years of visibility. By end market:

  • Liquid packaging board (about 21% to 23% of sales). Demand is stable and tied to milk and juice. Volumes for 2026 were contracted in line with 2025 under multi-year contracts. This is the one high-visibility leg.
  • European cartonboard and consumer and luxury board. Weak, with soft private consumption, down-trading to private label and plastic, and about 800,000 tonnes of oversupply. No recovery is visible.
  • Sack paper. Cement and building materials are 42% of volumes. European sack shipments in 2025 were 11% below 2021, despite +2.5% in 2025. This is held down by interest rates. Recovery depends on construction, which is not visible before 2027.
  • Containerboard, fluting and kraftliner. Better: semichemical fluting is sold out and European virgin kraftliner is tight. The tightness comes from supply (North American closures, US exports to Europe down 33%), not from demand.
  • North America. Solid and tariff-protected. Shipments in Q2 2026 were the highest since late 2022. But graphic paper (about 22% of group sales) is in a structural decline of 5% to 6.5% a year.

Group shipments rose to 903kt in Q3 2026 from 870kt (+3.8%), and European volumes were up 7% sequentially. But in Q1 2026 customers pre-bought ahead of price rises, and then destocking ended. On the "use, refill or price" test (Wisdom Library), the 2026 improvement is refill plus price, not use. DNB Carnegie models European cartonboard and containerboard demand growth of about 1% a year for 2026 to 2028. Brand owners have slowed plastic-to-paper substitution because of cost. Structurally, fibre substitution (PPWR from August 2026, about 3% a year of extra volume) roughly offsets the graphic-paper decline in revenue terms, but not in mix. For the model: assume group volume growth of 0% to 2% a year for 2027 and 2028. The skew is negative for Europe (risk of a second-half seasonal dip, with Jefferies expecting lower European volumes in Q4) and slightly positive for North American packaging.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F4322nd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three disruption forces act on Billerud. Their net effect over the next 6 to 18 months is mildly negative, because the force that hurts (digital substitution of graphic paper) runs faster and is more certain than the force that helps (plastic-to-fibre substitution).

  1. Plastic-to-fibre substitution. This is strengthening in law: PPWR applies from 12 August 2026, the PFAS ban in food-contact packaging, EPR fees, single-use plastic bans in more than 70 countries, and 84% to 89% of European consumers preferring fibre. But it is being slowed in practice by cost, by down-trading and by barrier-performance limits (many paper alternatives still need plastic lamination, and some brands have switched back). The industry has cut long-term cartonboard growth expectations back to historical averages. It acts positively but slowly, as an "early rather than big" story (sector digest).
  2. Digitalisation of graphic paper. This is confirmed and persistent, at a 5% to 6.5% annual decline. It acts negatively on about 22% of group sales, which are the North American base that funds the transition (North America made about 50% of group EBITDA in 2025).
  3. Lightweighting and grade convergence. This cuts both ways. New Billerud Flute (up to 25% lighter boxes) wins share but reduces tonnes per box. Grade convergence (SBS producers moving into recycled applications, and Asian virgin board closing the quality gap) erodes the boundaries that protect premium grades.

The force is being neither disproved nor accelerated. It acts on the company as a slow volume headwind in North America that the Evolution programme (300kt of packaging by 2030, against 11kt in Q3 2026) must outrun.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3416th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: regulatory change is substantial and, unusually for this sector, carbon rules have hit Billerud harder than its fossil-fuelled peers. 2026 is the worst year. 2027 should be better if the Swedish ETS1 amendment passes. From 2028 there is a structural cost again (ETS2, with no free allowances). The single biggest swing factor is legislative timing, not markets.

  • Carbon. Billerud's Swedish mills are more than 95% fossil-free, so they lost eligibility for free allocation in 2026. Two effects followed: the loss of surplus-allowance income (about SEK 507m net in 2025, Jefferies) and the payment of Swedish fossil carbon tax (about SEK 57m of carbon tax in Q3 2026; Q2 net emission effect SEK -16m against +120m a year earlier). A Swedish proposal brings Billerud back into ETS1 from 1 January 2027. One version is retroactive to 1 January 2026, which could change 2026 accounts. Under ETS2 there are no free allowances and purchases are needed from 2028.
  • United States. Tariffs from August 2025 (15% to 25%) protect the Michigan mills and divert customers to domestic supply. But they redirect global board flows into Europe, which adds to European oversupply. Direct exports from Europe to the US are only 2% of sales.
  • PPWR and EPR. A long-term demand support.
  • EUDR. A cost, but over 96% of Billerud's European wood is certified, which is a relative advantage against unintegrated buyers. The deadline has been delayed by one year.
  • Swedish forestry policy. LULUCF and the Nature Restoration Law will make wood harder and more expensive to procure. This is structural and negative.
  • The Russian wood ban. It removed about 10% of Nordic pulpwood supply permanently. This is the root of the structural wood cost problem.

The net effect on 2027 against 2026 is probably positive by SEK 0.1bn to 0.3bn if ETS1 re-entry passes. Against 2025 it is still negative, because surplus-allowance sales do not return. The Consumer must model carbon as a separate line with three dated states: 2026, 2027 and 2028 onward.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

D6347th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the sector's strongest current driver, the scarcity of fresh-fibre kraft paper, is accelerating Billerud's realised prices more than its volumes. Billerud is named the "purest way to own the story" (sector digest, 1 November 2026). The driver runs on a 12 to 24 month clock and is not a demand acceleration.

The mechanism: no new fresh-fibre machine is planned in Europe, North American closures removed about 10% of capacity, and US exports to Europe fell 33% in 2026 to August. So virgin kraftliner, sack and specialty kraft are scarce. Meanwhile recycled grades get new supply and higher gas costs. Evidence:

  • Kraftliner is up EUR 200 a tonne from the Q1 2026 low, with a EUR 230 premium over testliner.
  • Sack prices rose EUR 50 a tonne into Q4 2026.
  • Semichemical fluting is sold out.
  • European containerboard and sack prices rose in Q3 2026 ("price increases in fluting and containerboard pushed European margins to 12%").

The sector note judges that fresh-fibre mills can keep raising kraftliner and sack prices for roughly 12 to 24 months, and that a mill selling paper keeps more of a rise than a group selling boxes (box prices follow three to six months later). The first dated threat is UK recycled capacity from March 2027.

Secondary drivers: US tariffs, which accelerate domestic qualification of Tribute coated liner (Q3 2026 packaging volumes of 11kt, up from 9kt), and PPWR. Both are slow. Implication for the model: price, not volume, carries 2027 revenue growth. Consensus revenue growth of +4.2% in 2027 is roughly 2% to 3% price plus 1% to 2% volume, which is consistent with this driver if it is not reversed after mid-2027.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F4515th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand supports a stabilisation thesis, not a growth thesis. Order books are "very solid", destocking is over and LPB is stable, but the volume gains of 2026 include refill and pre-buying. The support is fragile in Europe and robust in North America.

Evidence:

  • Billerud's order books were "very solid" going into Q3 2026, with stocking from the Middle East disruption "very limited".
  • European customers pre-bought in Q1 2026 ahead of price rises, then normalised.
  • Q3 2026 shipments rose to 903kt from 870kt. European volumes rose 7% sequentially, but Jefferies expects a seasonal fall in Q4.
  • LPB volumes for 2026 were contracted flat on 2025.
  • North American customers favour domestic supply for security of supply and lead times. North American shipments in Q2 2026 were the highest since late 2022.
  • Brand owners have slowed paperisation, and lower-income consumers trade down to cheaper substrates (Graphic Packaging) and to plastic-packed discount goods.
  • European purchasing directors project only a moderate recovery in paper packaging demand into 2027.

For the model: European volume of about +1% to +2% in 2027 with a downside skew, and North American volume flat in total (packaging up, graphic down). Fragility is highest in luxury and consumer cartonboard and in sack, because of construction.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F3718th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators have hurt Billerud more than its peers in 2026 and may help in 2027. The position is fragile because the main relief, the Swedish ETS1 re-entry, is a proposal, not law.

  • EU and Sweden on carbon. The ETS excluded Billerud's fossil-free Swedish mills from free allocation, costing surplus-allowance income (about SEK 507m in 2025) and imposing a fossil carbon tax in 2026. The Swedish Ministry of Climate and Enterprise has proposed re-entry to ETS1 from 1 January 2027 (one draft retroactive to 1 January 2026). Under ETS2 there are no free allowances from 2028. Separately, the ETS benchmark update may cut free allocations by up to 50% across 2026 to 2030 for the sector.
  • EU packaging. PPWR (in force August 2026) helps. EUDR (delayed one year; fines of up to 4% of turnover) raises tracking costs but favours certified, large buyers like Billerud (over 96% certified).
  • US. The August 2025 tariffs help the Michigan mills.
  • Swedish forest policy. LULUCF and the Nature Restoration Law raise wood procurement costs over time. They hurt.

Fragility: high for carbon, where timing and retroactivity are uncertain; medium for US tariffs, which are political; low for PPWR. The Consumer should model carbon explicitly by year, with a sensitivity of roughly ±SEK 150m to 300m a year depending on the ETS outcome.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F359th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Billerud's addressable markets are large relative to its tonnage but slow-growing: about 1% to 3% a year in its packaging grades, offset by about -5% in graphic paper. Over the mid term the market allows group volume growth of roughly 0% to 2% a year. Revenue growth above that must come from price and mix, not market growth.

The relevant markets:

  • global LPB of about 4Mt (Billerud a meaningful share), stable to low growth;
  • European sack of 1.7Mt (still 11% below the 2021 peak in bag volumes);
  • coated white fresh-fibre liner under 1Mt globally;
  • Western European consumer board of about 17Mt (folding boxboard about 4Mt);
  • North American virgin paperboard growing 1.8% a year, where Billerud targets 300kt (small shares);
  • global paper and board heading to 519Mt by 2035;
  • European fibre-based packaging growing 3% to 4% a year to 2040 (Stora Enso's estimate);
  • the plastic-to-paper opportunity, about USD 5bn in North America and Europe (Morgan Stanley).

North American graphic paper (about 22% of group sales) declines 5% to 6.5% a year.

Consensus revenue growth of 4.2% (2027), 3.2% (2028) and 4.5% (2029, one broker) assumes a price contribution on top of 1% to 2% volume. That is plausible in 2027 and optimistic thereafter.

1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0660C-1054
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C-6053rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Billerud's own cycle turned at Q1 2026 and is in the early, price-and-cost-led phase of recovery. Revenue growth turned positive in Q3 2026, and earnings roughly doubled from Q1 to Q3. But revenue has not yet turned on volume, and year-on-year selling prices were still lower in Q3 2026.

FactSet quarterly revenue growth (year on year) ran -19.5% (Q4 2025), -13.8% (Q1 2026), -3.5% (Q2) and +2.9% (Q3). Adjusted EBITDA ran 818, 525, 661 and 1,032. The Q3 currency-neutral sales gain of 2% came from higher volumes offsetting lower year-on-year prices (the Change Forces report and the Earnings Review describe the price effect differently; sequentially, prices rose). Management guides about 1% sequential price rises in both regions for Q4 2026, continuing into H1 2027, along with a much smaller maintenance impact.

The turn is real in earnings but shallow in demand. The Q3 improvement came from four things: cost savings (SEK 150m a quarter), lower wood, European price and mix (Europe's margin reached 12%, the best since Q1 2025), and volume (+7% sequentially in Europe). Customers' pre-buying in Q1 2026 and the end of destocking flatter the volume comparison. The cycle is turning, but the turn needs (a) price rises to stick through Q1 2027 and (b) no wood reversal. The test is the Q4 2026 report on 2 February 2027: a Region Europe EBITDA margin below 10% would challenge the recovery thesis (the Earnings Review's own tripwire).

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

D5447th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is a moderate net tailwind for the next six to twelve months, carried almost entirely by the price-cost spread: scarce fresh-fibre grades plus lagged Swedish wood relief, with energy insulation. It becomes neutral to adverse in months 12 to 24, as three balancing forces arrive on dated clocks: the end of storm-salvage wood supply, new UK recycled capacity from March 2027, and krona strength as hedges roll off in Q2 2027.

Driver by driver:

  • Fresh-fibre scarcity: a strong tailwind for 12 to 24 months, over about 60% of the mix.
  • Nordic wood deflation: a strong tailwind in 2026, neutral in 2027 on management's guidance, with reversal risk from H2 2027.
  • European cartonboard and FBB overcapacity: a persistent headwind (Oulu, Chinese exports), about 7% to 15% of the mix directly, but it sets the tone for price negotiations.
  • Plastic-to-fibre regulation: a weak but durable tailwind.
  • Graphic paper digitalisation: a steady headwind on about 22% of sales.
  • Carbon regulation: a headwind in 2026, partly relieved in 2027, a headwind again from 2028.
  • The krona: a headwind, and the largest macro item.
  • US protectionism: a tailwind for North America, and a mild headwind for European flows.
  • Energy costs: a relative tailwind.
  • Consolidation: an optional tailwind with no date.

At value-chain level, supplier power (forest owners) is temporarily weakened by the wood surplus, but it is structural and will reassert itself. Customer power is high in oversupplied grades, where "customers take a cost saving back", and low in scarce grades. At industry level, North America is consolidated and disciplined while Europe is fragmented and caught in a prisoner's dilemma.

Overall: the external forces explain why 2026 is the trough and why earnings rise into H1 2027. They do not support a mid-cycle EBITDA of SEK 6bn by 2028. The cone for 2027 is skewed to the downside of consensus: tailwinds are mostly in the run-rate already, and headwinds are dated and still ahead.

Internal change forces/risks

D5830th percentile

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Billerud is a credible self-help story with an operator management team, a proven record in cost programmes and genuine capital discipline. That makes the controllable part of the 2027 earnings bridge (about SEK 0.25bn of incremental savings, SEK 0.1bn to 0.2bn of maintenance normalisation, and steady OEE gains) among the most dependable inputs in the model. Strategy was reset well in 2024: value over volume, no large conversions, regional targets. Since then management has executed rather than re-planned. The limits are just as clear:

  • the internal changes do not address the structural cost disadvantage (no forest, SEK cost base) or European overcapacity;
  • shareholder returns are being cut, not raised;
  • the Gävle boiler is an unresolved claim on cash;
  • turnover below the top two and recurring North American shutdown overruns are real execution risks.

The corporate changes are necessary for recovery but not sufficient for the SEK 6bn ambition. The next decisive corporate signal would be a European closure or consolidation step, which is mooted but not dated.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement35C-1460C-8645C-5958C-8135D-0340
Group judgement

Risks

D3527th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is dominated by a few large, correlated variables (Nordic wood, the krona, European board pricing) acting on a highly operationally levered earnings base. The share price has already moved from trough book value to an early-recovery multiple. Revenue has bottomed and the earnings engine is accelerating, so a near-term deceleration is not the main risk. The main risk is the set of 2027 balancing forces:

  • a wood rebound after salvage felling ends;
  • krona strength as hedges roll off;
  • price give-back in FY2027 renewals.

Any one of them can remove SEK 0.3bn to 0.9bn of EBITDA. Taken together they would cut EPS by more than half. Sentiment risk is high because Billerud is the sector's purest proxy for one story, with a dated first test (March 2027), in a risk-off regime. A fair trough is about SEK 58 to 67 (-25% to -35%), with a tail of SEK 45 to 50, against about 9% upside to the mean target. Balance-sheet risk is low.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C-6053rd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: cost discipline is the clearest improvement and has a record behind it. Operational execution at the mills, particularly in North America, is the clearest weakness. Governance is sound.

Cost.

  • The SEK 1.5bn efficiency programme (about 190 work streams) closed at the end of 2024, one year early. It was started under the previous chief executive.
  • The SEK 800m programme (launched 18 September 2025; up to 650 positions; SEK 350m restructuring charge in Q3 2025) completed all staff reductions by early April 2026. It delivered SEK 100m in Q1 and SEK 150m in each of Q2 and Q3 2026, is on track for SEK 550m in 2026, and reaches the full SEK 800m run-rate from 2027. The permanent workforce is 15% smaller over three years.
  • Capex discipline: two cuts to guidance.
  • Supporting systems: the Billerud Production System has been rolled out across European board mills (OEE target of +2 points a year), a global ERP harmonisation is under way (Gävle in 2026), and all mills hold ISO 9001 (Quinnesec in Q3 2026).

Execution weaknesses.

  • The Quinnesec shutdown overran its budget by about SEK 50m (Q2 2026, delayed start-up), and the Escanaba shutdown cost SEK 104m against SEK 90m (Q3 2026), with lingering inefficiencies. North America's Q3 EBITDA missed consensus by 13%.
  • Lost-time injuries (5.5 in 2024 against a target of 2.5) and the 2023 blastomycosis outbreak at Escanaba.

Governance. Every board member is independent of management and six of seven are independent of major owners. There is a Speak-Up line and eight board policies.

The pattern for the model: assume savings deliver to plan, and assume North American maintenance and ramp-ups carry a SEK 50m to 100m a year overrun risk.

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

B+4584th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negative corporate developments are moderate and mostly about execution and capital, not integrity.

  1. Leadership churn below the top two. EVP Legal & Strategic Projects leaves at the end of 2026; North America has had two presidents in under two years; the sustainability head has changed. This threatens continuity on strategic projects.
  2. Strain on the workforce. A 15% headcount cut in three years; the 2025 survey shows equal stress across white-collar and blue-collar staff; turnover rose to 8.7% (November 2026) from 8.1%, and from 7.7% (2023). Safety is below target (LTIFR 5.5 against 2.5).
  3. Recurring North American execution overruns at shutdowns and ramp-ups. These are the same assets that carry the Evolution transition.
  4. Capital pressure. The Gävle boiler (above SEK 3bn) alongside base capex of about SEK 2.2bn and Evolution's SEK 0.46bn in 2027 could push capex above SEK 3bn a year in 2028 to 2029 and squeeze dividends.
  5. A credibility gap in North America between the "Evolution progress" message and a US expert's "wild card / cancelled" perception. Clearwater describes an oversupplied SBS market, against management's "solid North American conditions".
  6. Weaker communication on returns. The dividend cut surprised the market, and no insider bought shares in the open market at the trough.
  7. Earnings quality. Supplier finance arrangements with payment terms of up to 127 days support working capital, so cash conversion is partly financed by suppliers.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C5866th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: the system is reasonably congruent around a cost-and-discipline culture. Goals (ROCE, cash conversion, EBITDA), strategy (value over volume), incentives (short-term pay on ROCE, operating cash flow and safety; long-term share programmes on TSR, earnings and a sustainability condition, with participants required to invest in shares) and decisions (cancelled capex, cost cuts) point the same way. It is not a Lifco- or Ryanair-style integrated culture with an outlier identity.

Points of incongruence:

  1. The workforce culture is under strain after the 15% cuts, with equal stress among white-collar and blue-collar staff and rising turnover.
  2. Safety targets are missed, at more than double the target.
  3. The innovation and customer co-creation message (BoxLab, PharmaKraft, Flute) sits alongside a cost-out culture, which researchers describe as possibly "bureaucratic cost-out" rather than high-performance.
  4. North America's frequent leadership changes suggest it is less integrated into the group culture.

The strongest congruence is in capital allocation: the board and management have shown they will walk away from projects that do not meet return criteria.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

F3525th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the sudden fundamental risks cluster around Billerud's two structural exposures (Nordic wood and the krona) and its concentration in a few mills, grades and customers. I list them by plausibility over 18 months, with the size as an EBITDA effect:

  1. Swedish wood rebounds after salvage felling ends, or from a new supply shock (for example, a policy curb on felling or a Baltic logistics problem). Plausibility: about 25% to 30% for a rise of 10% or more by H2 2027. Size: about SEK 0.9bn per 10%, after a three to six month lag. This is the largest risk.
  2. A krona surge (EUR/SEK at 10.40 or below, USD weakness) as hedges roll off in Q2 2027. Plausibility: about 20%. Size: about SEK 0.3bn to 0.6bn. DNB Carnegie says this would neutralise the SEK 900m of wood relief.
  3. A European price attack in FBB or LPB by Stora Enso (filling Oulu) or Metsä Board (distressed), or Asian exporters gaining LPB share. Plausibility: about 15% to 20%. Size: SEK 0.2bn to 0.8bn (a 5% to 10% move in LPB price alone is SEK 0.4bn to 0.8bn).
  4. Loss or shrinkage of an LPB contract with Tetra Pak, SIG or Elopak (the top five customers are about one-third of sales). Plausibility: about 5% to 10%, given multi-year contracts and qualification. Size: large, because LPB is about 21% to 23% of revenue and the most margin-critical grade.
  5. The Swedish ETS1 re-entry fails or is delayed, or retroactive terms are adverse. Plausibility: about 20% to 25%. Size: about SEK 0.1bn to 0.3bn a year.
  6. A serious mill incident or extended outage. Precedents: the Karlsborg fire (SEK 50m, 2024), the Escanaba blastomycosis outbreak (2023), Metsä's Kemi explosion (EUR 40m). Plausibility: about 15% a year. Size: SEK 0.05bn to 0.5bn.
  7. US tariffs removed or absorbed by exporters, or graphic paper decline accelerating. Plausibility: about 10% to 15%. Size: SEK 0.2bn to 0.5bn in North America.
  8. Gävle approved at above SEK 3bn alongside weak earnings, forcing a dividend freeze. Plausibility: about 25%. This is a valuation and sentiment hit rather than an EBITDA one.

Risks 1 to 3 are correlated with each other through the same European cycle, so a joint adverse outcome (EBITDA of SEK 3.5bn to 3.9bn in 2027 instead of 4.6bn) is plausible at roughly one in six.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

D4034th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the drivers are diffuse in what they are expected to add and concentrated in what could go wrong. The expected rise in EBITDA from FY2026 to FY2027 (about SEK 1.1bn) is spread over about ten items, and four controllable ones supply about two-fifths of it. But three variables set outside the company (selling price, Nordic wood and the krona) carry about 85% of the variance around that rise. The concentration is tighter still on three counts: by grade (LPB and the containerboard and kraft grades), by region (Europe carries all of the wood and carbon and most of the currency), and by time (about 95% of the 2027 increase falls in first-half comparisons). Against the last four to six years this is the same business moved by the same five variables. What is new is that self-help now supplies a large, dependable part of the change.

The expected change, by source (my amended bridge, SEKm).

Source Expected Share of the increase Plausible range
Controllable: savings 250, maintenance 100, North American non-recurrence 50, OEE 50 +450 42% +300 to +550
Price, net of non-wood and wage inflation (850 less 425) +425 40% +100 to +700
Nordic wood +150 14% -300 to +600
Volume +100 9% -150 to +250
Krona -50 -5% -250 to +150
Carbon 0 0% -50 to +50
Total about +1,075

Treating the ranges as independent, price, wood and the krona account for about 85% of the variance and volume for most of the rest. The controllable items account for under 5%.

How this compares with the last four to six years. In each year since 2021, two or three variables explained most of the change in EBITDA, and across the whole period only five variables did the work:

  • FY2022 (EBITDA up about SEK 4.3bn on FactSet's operating basis): price and mix, and the Verso acquisition.
  • FY2023 (down about SEK 4.3bn): customer destocking and mill curtailment, wood, and price.
  • FY2024 (up about SEK 1.4bn): price recovery led by LPB, and the cost programme (SEK 840m).
  • FY2025 (down about SEK 1.1bn): wood at its peak, the krona and European volume.
  • FY2026 (down about SEK 0.7bn on the adjusted basis): the loss of allowance income (about SEK 0.5bn) and lower prices, against wood relief and savings in the second half.

The forecast average for FY2027 to FY2030 (EBITDA about SEK 4.9bn) differs from the FY2023 to FY2025 average (about SEK 4.2bn) by only two lasting items of opposite sign, the banked cost programmes and the lost allowance income, plus a cyclical recovery in the spread. The forecast does not introduce a new driver.

The insight that matters for predictability: the bad years are the years when wood and price decouple. When demand moves, wood and selling prices move the same way and partly offset each other in the spread. The collapse of 2023 to 2025 happened because they separated: wood rose for a supply reason (the loss of Russian imports) while prices fell for supply and inventory reasons. The main adverse case for the second half of 2027 has the same structure. Wood would rise for a supply reason (the end of storm salvage) while containerboard and cartonboard prices are pressed for a supply reason (1.45Mt of UK recycled capacity and Stora Enso's Oulu line reaching full output by the end of 2027). Weak demand does not hedge that combination. It is also why the product spread across eight lines diversifies less than it appears to.

Where there are real offsets. Region North America (about 40% of regional EBITDA in the forecast) has local wood, dollar costs and tariff protection, and its two risks (tariff changes and the 5% annual decline in graphic paper) are unrelated to the European ones. High gas prices raise recycled rivals' costs and support the virgin premium. And a rise in wood removes buyers' argument for price cuts, so wood and the LPB give-back do not add up in full.

Concentration in time. On the second set's quarterly path, EBITDA is up SEK 638m in Q1 2027 and SEK 426m in Q2 against a year earlier, then up SEK 87m in Q3 and down SEK 25m in Q4. The whole year's growth is two comparisons against the abnormally weak first half of 2026.

What this means for the Consumer. A case driven by a few variables with public prices is simple to state and to follow, which is a point in its favour. But its forecast error is not diversified. One variable can move the year: 10% on Nordic wood is about SEK 0.9bn of EBITDA, or about SEK 2.8 of EPS against a forecast of SEK 4.4; 1% on group price is about SEK 1.25 of EPS.

Internal corporate change forces

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

C+5868th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the top two are stable and internal: chief executive Ivar Vatne (acting from July 2023, permanent from December 2023, previously CFO and deputy chief executive) and CFO Andrei Krés (at Billerud since 2011). The layer below has been almost completely replaced, with external operators from UPM, International Paper and Sonoco. The signal is a deliberate shift from a transformation-and-growth regime (the Michalski era: the Verso acquisition and the SEK 7bn Escanaba conversion) to an operator-led regime of cost, asset efficiency and value over volume. The imported UPM know-how, in a company now openly evaluating European closures, hints at harder footprint decisions ahead.

The changes:

  • Board. Magnus Nicolin became chairman (AGM May 2026; on the board since 2022, so continuity). Bernd Eikens joined (formerly at UPM, now chief executive of Meyer Shipyards).
  • Regions. Jaakko Nikkilä became President Europe (May 2025; formerly head of UPM Specialty Papers, replacing Matthew Hirst). Doug Schwartz became President North America (September 2024; from International Paper and Sonoco, after Kevin Kuznicki left suddenly).
  • Operations. Tor Lundqvist returned as SVP Operations Europe.
  • Sustainability. Sofia Hedevåg replaced Ulrika Wedberg as EVP Sustainability.
  • Legal and strategic projects. EVP Legal & Strategic Projects Andreas Mattsson leaves by 31 December 2026 to become Chief Legal Officer at Essity. His successor search is ongoing.

Andreas Mattsson's departure matters more than it looks: he ran strategic projects, which is the function that would execute any M&A, closure or Gävle decision. Major shareholders are AMF Pension (15.6%) and Frapag (12.1%, represented on the board by Florian Heiserer). There is no activist and no sign of owner pressure.

The negative signal is the frequency of departures (two regional presidents and several sustainability heads within two years). Leadership researchers flag it as possible friction, and it raises execution risk during a period of restructuring.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C-6353rd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy changed decisively in 2024 and has been stable since. "The Way Forward" (Capital Markets Day, December 2024) puts profitability and capital efficiency over volume, accepts capacity reduction "when beneficial", and replaces a transformational US conversion with incremental upgrades. It is clearly articulated, with regional EBITDA targets, and adequately but modestly resourced. It is a sensible defensive choice that caps growth: there is no European growth engine beyond product mix.

The choices:

  • The SEK 7bn (USD 1bn) Escanaba cartonboard conversion was cancelled in May 2024 on cost inflation and returns.
  • It was replaced by the SEK 1.3bn Evolution programme for 2024 to 2027 (SEK 1.1bn Escanaba, SEK 0.2bn Quinnesec; SEK 510m spent by 2025, SEK 330m in 2026, SEK 460m in 2027). The programme aims at 300kt of North American packaging by 2030, while running graphic paper for cash.
  • Europe is run for asset efficiency, with a standardised production system, OEE, the cost programme, and price over volume.
  • The new targets: EBITDA above SEK 6bn over a cycle (Europe SEK 4.3bn to 4.8bn, North America SEK 2bn to 2.5bn); EBITDA margin above 15% (lowered from 17% for structurally higher wood); ROCE above 11%; cash conversion above 80%; net debt to EBITDA below 2.5x.

Resourcing: capex of SEK 2.4bn (2026) and SEK 2.6bn (2027) including SEK 0.5bn strategic, falling to a base of about SEK 2.2bn after 2027. The weakness is that strategy depends on external closures in Europe to reach the European target, and on a seven-fold volume ramp in North America. Jefferies calls the European target unrealistic and the North American one achievable.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

D4430th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there is no annual financial guidance to raise. The mid-term targets were reset in December 2024: the margin target was lowered (17% to 15%), and an absolute SEK 6bn EBITDA ambition and a re-introduced ROCE target were added. Since then the only raises are operational: 2026 savings went from SEK 500m to SEK 550m, and planned headcount reductions were completed ahead of schedule. Guidance on spending has been cut (2025 capex SEK 3.5bn to 3.1bn; 2026 SEK 2.6bn to 2.4bn), which is positive for cash, not earnings.

The direction of travel in ambition is down in percentage terms and steady in absolute terms. The SEK 6bn target sits above everything except the 2022 peak. Reported FactSet operating EBITDA was 7,887 in 2022, 5,024 in 2024 and 3,922 in 2025; on the brokers' basis it was 5,323 in 2024 and 4,098 in 2025. The targets are stated "over a cycle" without a date (one Jefferies note frames 2027 to 2030). Nothing has been raised that changes the earnings path. The one implicit near-term commitment, the SEK 800m run-rate from 2027, is the most bankable item in the case.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

C5361st percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Billerud has steadily trimmed small, non-core pieces but has not yet taken the decision that would matter: a permanent European capacity closure. It now says openly that it is evaluating closures and M&A, so the perimeter question is live but unresolved.

Actions taken:

  • The Managed Packaging business was divested (August 2023).
  • The idled Wisconsin Rapids mill was sold (mid-2024; about SEK 60m of cash).
  • The Wisconsin Water Quality Center and landfill were divested (April 2024; +SEK 70m item affecting comparability).
  • The Viken Skog Follum BCTMP joint venture was exited (Q1 2026; write-off of about SEK 47m to 50m), removing a future capex commitment.
  • The SEK 7bn conversion was cancelled.
  • Simplification: two regions (August 2023), Billerud Europe AB centralising sales and logistics (Q4 2025), and a captive reinsurer (licensed Q2 2026).

What has not happened: any closure of a European machine in oversupplied cartonboard or pulp, or a decision on North American graphic paper machines beyond running them for cash. Jefferies' downgrade (29 September 2026) was explicitly "Recovery or Strategic Actions Needed to Turn Constructive". A closure would lower volume but raise margins and the industry's operating rates. For the model: assume no closure in the base case, but treat a European closure announcement as a positive scenario in the next 6 to 18 months, with restructuring cash costs.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D5539th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the entries are sensible, low-capital and adjacent, but small relative to the group. The main one is North American virgin packaging board, made on existing graphic-paper machines with low-cost local fibre behind a tariff wall. The risk is pace, not logic.

  • North America. Tribute coated white kraftliner and Voyager are produced at Quinnesec and Escanaba. More than 70 customer trials have been run, and the first commercial orders were won from domestic corrugated plants. North American containerboard and cartonboard sales grew seven-fold to SEK 91m in Q2 2026. Packaging volumes were 9kt in Q2 and 11kt in Q3 2026. The target is 300kt by 2030 (Jefferies elsewhere cites 320kt), so volume must rise about seven-fold from today's annualised run-rate of about 44kt. The economics are sound: hardwood chips at about USD 85 a cubic metre against USD 140 in Sweden, removals of 5.1 million against growth of 12.6 million cubic metres in the Michigan basin, shorter lead times than importers, and a 15% to 25% import tariff. Grade targets imply small shares: 2% of US cartonboard and 7% of white-top liner.
  • Europe. Product launches rather than markets: PharmaKraft (October 2026, dry pharmaceutical packaging), New Billerud Flute (October 2026; up to 25% lighter boxes from the PM6 headbox at Gruvön), the PM8 Yankee cylinder at Skärblacka for medical and hygiene paper, and ConFlex HeatSeal.

The US SBS market is oversupplied, and one US expert questioned whether the conversion was continuing. That gap between the company's narrative and how customers see it is the thing to monitor. For the model: North American packaging of about 60kt to 100kt in 2027 and 100kt to 160kt in 2028, below a straight line to 300kt.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

D4537th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: across the group, Billerud's price rises are nominal and cost-driven. Year-on-year selling prices were still lower in Q2 and Q3 2026, and the improvement in spread came from falling wood, not from price. Real rises, above cost inflation, exist only in the scarce grades (kraftliner, sack, semichemical fluting) and in tariff-protected North America. In cartonboard the opposite is happening: cost relief is being given back.

Evidence:

  • Management calls them "cost-driven price increases" to offset Middle East logistics and chemical inflation. It guides about 1% sequential price in both regions for Q4 2026, with positive momentum into H1 2027. These rises are nominal: they offset SEK 60m of Q4 input-cost inflation.
  • Real rises: unbleached kraftliner up EUR 200 a tonne from its Q1 low and sack up EUR 50 a tonne into Q4 2026, both well above cost inflation; a price-cost spread "particularly visible in niche virgin containerboard and high-quality sack papers" (Jefferies); US domestic prices up 11% in H1 2026 after closures, and Billerud raised North American graphic and label prices in Q2 2026.
  • Latent: LPB contracts were repriced upwards (+8% to 9% in early 2024) with escalation clauses and indexation added. They reprice with a lag, and a 5% to 10% LPB price move is worth SEK 0.4bn to 0.8bn.
  • Absent: in cartonboard, "European producers often give back wood cost relief" (Jefferies), and low utilisation means no pricing power (DNB Carnegie).

The decisive evidence on pricing power is the 2021 to 2025 period. Wood doubled and the industry could not pass it through: European EBIT margin fell to 3% in 2025. For the model: about +2% to +3% average price in 2027 against 2026, of which perhaps half is real. Price should not be treated as a durable margin driver after mid-2027.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D5035th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Billerud is not stepping up M&A. It is mooting it ("on the table", "evaluating all options including closures and M&A") as a tool to address European overcapacity, with no target or timing. Its last major deal (Verso, 2022) looks strategically sound in hindsight on earnings, if not on returns.

The record:

  • Korsnäs (2012) built the LPB and sack franchise.
  • Verso (2022; about USD 825m) added Escanaba and Quinnesec. Region North America produced about 50% of group EBITDA in 2025 on one-third of sales, with 16% to 22% EBITDA margins through recent cycles and a fibre-cost advantage, and it now underpins the tariff-protected packaging option. It was penalised at the time on dilution and graphic-paper exposure, and the follow-on conversion plan had to be cancelled. Integration produced a US pension settlement gain (SEK 389m, 2024) and transformation costs (SEK 278m, 2024), and two North American presidents in two years.

There is no evidence of M&A discipline failure. There is also no evidence of an active pipeline. If European consolidation happens, Billerud is as likely to be a participant through asset swaps or closures as a buyer. The balance sheet (2.0x) would allow a modest deal, but Gävle (above SEK 3bn) competes for the same capital. For the model: no M&A in the base case.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F304th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: shareholder returns are stepping down, not up. The dividend for 2025 was cut to SEK 2.00 (from SEK 3.50; consensus had been SEK 3.30 to 3.50). There have been no buybacks in 2024 to 2026. The 2024 debate about an "overcapitalised balance sheet" and excess cash for distributions has gone quiet. The next capital call is the Gävle recovery boiler (above SEK 3bn, decision in 2027), which Jefferies says could force management to suspend dividend growth.

The policy is to distribute at least 50% of net profit (the 2025 payout was about 70% on reported net profit of SEK 711m). Leverage is 2.0x at Q3 2026 (company basis; FactSet 2.31x including leases), peaking at about 1.7x to 2.2x in 2026 on depressed EBITDA, against a ceiling of 2.5x. It should fall towards 1.1x to 1.5x in 2027 on consensus EBITDA. Jefferies notes buybacks are deferred until earnings approach cycle targets.

Consensus DPS (FactSet period basis) is SEK 2.00 for 2026, then 2.62 and 3.12. DNB Carnegie has SEK 1.75 for FY2026 and SEK 2.75 for FY2027. For the model: a dividend of about SEK 1.75 to 2.00 for FY2026, rising with earnings at a 50% to 60% payout. No buyback before 2028 unless Gävle is deferred again. Balance-sheet capacity is real (about SEK 3bn to 4bn of headroom to 2.5x on 2027 EBITDA), but management is using it for resilience and capex, not returns.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–57

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several smaller positive forces add up to perhaps SEK 0.2bn to 0.4bn a year of further improvement over 2027 to 2028, beyond the SEK 800m programme. All are already in management's plan and none is transformational.

  • OEE through the Billerud Production System. A target of +2 points a year, with 1 point worth about SEK 100m of EBITDA.
  • Wood procurement. More direct purchases from Swedish private owners (over 15% of the 2024 mix), the Sveaskog agreement, and continued cooperation with Viken Skog despite the JV exit.
  • Chemical contract renegotiations. SEK 130m of sequential relief in Q1 2026.
  • Mix upgrades. New Flute, PharmaKraft, PM8 specialty, and North American packaging that replaces declining graphic tonnes.
  • Process and system changes. ERP and process harmonisation, Billerud Europe AB, and the captive insurer, which lowers property-insurance cost.
  • Capex falling to a SEK 2.2bn base after 2027, which raises free cash flow if Gävle is phased.
  • Energy efficiency investment (Escanaba heat recovery, the Gävle boiler) that lowers energy use and fossil emissions, which matters under ETS2.

The plausible but unconfirmed force is a European capacity closure or consolidation, which would be the largest positive change if it came.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

C-5051st percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: hardly at all. The two largest holders, AMF Pension (15.6%) and Frapag Beteiligungsholding (12.1%, board seat via Florian Heiserer), have given no public sign of wanting change. The chairman transition (Svensson to Nicolin, a board member since 2022) is continuity. There is no activist on the register, and the last notable shareholder dissent dates from the 2022 Verso deal.

The only nudges come from the sell-side, not owners: Jefferies' call for "strategic actions" (closures and M&A), and the historical retail expectation of large capital returns after the Escanaba cancellation, which Jefferies judged low-credibility. Short interest doubled to 2.17% of free float after the H1 2025 miss, but that is positioning, not engagement. Owners are therefore neither a catalyst nor a constraint. Strategic change will come from management and the board responding to industry economics, most likely through European footprint decisions. For the model this means no owner-driven event risk in either direction over 18 months.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

D6034th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive corporate changes are strong in depth and narrow in breadth. They are concentrated in cost and capital discipline, where delivery is proven and the money is committed. They are thin in growth and in the one structural lever that would change mid-cycle economics, permanent European capacity reduction.

What is strong and resourced:

  • Self-help. SEK 800m of savings from 2027, after SEK 1.5bn by 2024, with all staff reductions done.
  • Capital discipline. The SEK 7bn conversion was cancelled, capex guidance was cut twice, and the JV was exited.
  • An operator team with UPM, International Paper and Sonoco experience.
  • A modest, low-risk North American packaging option.

What is broad but small: OEE, mix and procurement improvements, worth perhaps SEK 0.2bn to 0.4bn a year.

What is missing: a European footprint decision, a real growth engine in Europe, and any step-up in shareholder returns.

So the corporate changes can plausibly take EBITDA from about SEK 4.1bn (2025 base) to about SEK 4.9bn to 5.2bn over 2027 to 2028 in neutral markets: 0.8bn of savings, plus 0.2bn to 0.4bn of OEE and mix, less inflation. They cannot alone reach SEK 6bn. Wood, carbon and currency remain larger than anything management controls. The Consumer should model the controllable items with high confidence and treat everything else as external.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F3719th percentile

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the staircase is real but low in novelty. Most steps are mechanical confirmations of a recovery the market already expects. The only novel, high-impact steps (a European capacity closure or consolidation, or a step-up in shareholder returns) are undated and uncertain. Several dated steps are negative.

Within six months (to May 2027):

  • Q4 2026 results (2 February 2027). The first full quarter of lower Swedish wood in the P&L, the smaller maintenance impact, and +1% price. A European EBITDA margin of 10% or more confirms the thesis. Well-founded; moderately novel.
  • The FY2026 dividend proposal (about SEK 1.75 to 2.00). Low novelty.
  • Swedish ETS1 re-entry from 1 January 2027 (possibly retroactive). This would end the fossil carbon tax: well-founded but legislative, and novel for 2027 margins.
  • The SEK 800m savings run-rate reached (Q1 2027). Well-founded; not novel.
  • H1 2027 price rises in kraftliner and sack. Moderately founded.
  • A negative step: UK recycled capacity (King's Lynn) from March 2027.

Months 6 to 18 (May 2027 to May 2028):

  • Evolution completed at Escanaba and Quinnesec in 2027 (SEK 460m), with North American packaging volumes stepping up from about 11kt a quarter. Moderately novel; execution risk.
  • FY2027 EBITDA up 30% to 40% (Q1 to Q3 2027 reports). Expected.
  • Leverage falling towards 1.1x to 1.5x, which opens buyback or dividend-growth talk. Novel if it happens.
  • A possible European closure or M&A announcement. The most novel step, and undated.
  • The Gävle recovery boiler decision (above SEK 3bn), negative for cash.
  • The end of salvage felling and a risk of wood rising (negative).
  • EUDR compliance from December 2026, a relative positive.

Months 18 to 36 (2028 to 2029):

  • The North American packaging ramp towards 300kt by 2030. Novel, uncertain.
  • The SEK 6bn target tested. Low probability.
  • ETS2 purchases from 2028 (negative).
  • Gävle construction capex (negative for free cash flow).
  • PPWR recyclability mandates by 2030, a slow positive.

The market will look to the Q4 2026 and H1 2027 prints for confirmation. After that the staircase thins out, and novel positive news depends on management choosing to close capacity or return capital.
