Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

F4519th percentile

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

F4519th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Billerud occupies good positions (number one or two) in concentrated, qualified niches (LPB, sack, semichemical fluting, virgin kraftliner, North American coated paper). But it sits in the least profitable stages of a value chain whose ends, forest ownership and converting, capture the stable margins. The industry structure is supportive now because:

  • fresh fibre is scarce;
  • North America has consolidated and is tariff-protected;
  • Nordic suppliers have temporarily lost pricing power.

The structure is fragile because:

  • European exit barriers keep oversupply in place;
  • Stora Enso and Metsä Board have incentives to fill machines;
  • recycled conversions narrow the virgin premium from 2027;
  • forest owners regain pricing power when salvage felling ends.

Precedent says non-integrated Nordic mills outperform in the first year of a spread inflection, which is now largely in the share price, and then give up relative margin through the cycle. For the model: Billerud keeps price gains in about 60% of its mix through H1 2027, gives back in cartonboard and LPB renewals, and faces a narrower virgin premium in H2 2027 and 2028.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3245C-3338C-3444C-3737C-3835C-3935
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?F4515th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F388th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is a mild hindrance. Billerud sells upstream substrate into concentrated converter channels (Tetra Pak, SIG and Elopak in LPB; the five largest customers are about one-third of sales) and, in North America, into merchant distributors that hold pricing power in graphic paper. It does not own the higher-margin converting stage.

The value chain shows why:

  • Forestry earns 15% to 25% EBIT.
  • Paperboard mills earn 6% to 12% at mid-cycle.
  • LPB converting earns a stable 10% to 15% through the cycle.
  • Brand owners earn more than 15%. Billerud sits only in the two least attractive stages (pulp and board).

Converter concentration means contracts lag cost inflation and needed renegotiation and indexation clauses. In sack, Mondi's full integration into bags gives it a structural pricing edge. In North America, Billerud is selling packaging board directly to converters to bypass distributors, which helps the new business.

The support is moderately fragile: top-five concentration means losing or shrinking one LPB contract would be material (LPB is about 21% to 23% of revenue and the most margin-critical grade). The Risks report rates contractual LPB volume loss as its highest-plausibility failure mode, though I judge it lower probability than the report does, given multi-year contracts and long qualification. Credit risk is low: most receivables carry credit insurance.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D4439th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors' behaviour helps Billerud in kraft, sack and fluting and hurts it in cartonboard and LPB, and the balance between the two shifts against Billerud in 2027.

What competitors are doing:

  • Stora Enso is ramping Oulu, running a EUR 500m to 700m self-help programme, demerging its forests and closing Skutskär line 3.
  • Metsä Board closed Tako, is cutting EUR 200m of costs, has suspended its dividend and is redirecting tariff-hit US volume into Europe.
  • Mondi is squeezed by Central European wood and energy and is integrating Schumacher.
  • Smurfit Westrock and SAICA lead recycled containerboard price rises (+EUR 80 a tonne) and are closing Birmingham.
  • SCA and Holmen are disciplined and integrated.

What helps: Smurfit, SAICA and SCA leading containerboard prices up; Mondi leading sack prices; recycled rivals hurt by gas costs; Metsä Board losing US share to tariffs, which supports Billerud's domestic North American mills.

What hurts: Metsä Board's US volumes are redirected into Europe, Oulu fills folding boxboard, and Stora Enso, the LPB leader, can use its integration and patents to compete for renewals.

Billerud's position is robust against Mondi and Smurfit, because it is a supplier in a scarce grade. It is fragile against Stora Enso in LPB and cartonboard, and against Metsä Board in white-top kraftliner and FBB if Metsä prices for volume. A positive scenario, with a low probability of perhaps 15% to 20% over 18 months, is a competitor closure in FBB or kraft.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F3718th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F3510th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption acts at every point in Billerud's value chain, and the net effect is to leave it exposed at the two ends it does not own: its suppliers (wood) and its customers' customers (consumer down-trading).

By actor:

  • Demand (brand owners and consumers). Regulation pushes towards fibre, while inflation pushes towards cheaper substrates and private label. Digital media erodes graphic paper. Net: slow positive for fibre, steady negative for graphic paper.
  • Competition. Asian low-cost integrated capacity, recycled conversions in Europe, and grade convergence (SBS into recycled uses) all push intensity up.
  • Regulators and politicians. Carbon rules penalise frontrunners; tariffs regionalise trade; EUDR raises compliance cost and favours scale.
  • Suppliers (forest owners). The Russian ban gave Nordic forest owners structural pricing power. Storm felling has temporarily reversed it. Forestry policy restricts supply further. Chemical and logistics suppliers pass on Middle East costs.
  • Customers (converters). They are consolidating (Mondi-Schumacher, SAICA-Thimm, Smurfit-WestRock), which raises buyer power.

Billerud's position is fragile at the supplier end, which it cannot hedge (no forest), and in cartonboard. It is robust in North America (local fibre and tariffs) and in qualified LPB and sack.

C-39Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?F3522nd percentilefull answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
3b

Industry structure

10 answers
Judgement45C-2460C-2568C-2658C-2738C-2847C-2942C-3150C-3544C-3645
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)F4519th percentilefull answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

D6039th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Billerud is number one or two in the niches that make up most of its profit, and a mid-sized or small player in the commodity grades. That is the right shape for a non-integrated producer: it leads where qualification and fibre properties matter, and it is a price-taker where they do not.

Positions:

  • Liquid packaging board: global number two, behind Stora Enso, which supplies about one carton in three globally. Gruvön alone has about 870kt of LPB capacity.
  • European sack paper: number two, behind Mondi, the price-setter with 30% to 40%+ of capacity.
  • European semichemical fluting: number two, sold out.
  • European white-top kraftliner: number three, with about 14% (Metsä Board 32%, Smurfit Westrock 23%).
  • European folding boxboard: about 18% of capacity (Metsä Board 32%, Stora Enso 29%).
  • North American coated freesheet: number two, with about 41%.
  • North American packaging: a newcomer, targeting about 2% of US cartonboard and 7% of white-top liner by 2030.
  • Nordic wood: the largest buyer of Nordic pulpwood (about 10 million cubic metres a year), which makes it the largest price-taker, not a price-setter.

At group level, revenue of SEK 40.5bn (about EUR 3.6bn) is less than half of Stora Enso's (EUR 9.3bn) and about the same as Mayr-Melnhof's (EUR 3.9bn). Billerud is close to the leaders in its niches. The gap to number one in LPB is large (Stora Enso has more scale, more patents and integrated forest and pulp). In sack, Mondi's downstream bag integration gives it structurally better pricing.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C-6851st percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Billerud's core niches are concentrated and steep, well above the 30% top-five threshold and close to oligopolies, while its commodity grades are fragmented. Profit depends on the concentrated niches holding discipline.

  • LPB: the top ten hold 80% to 90% of global capacity, with Stora Enso about one-third of cartons and Billerud second. Very steep.
  • European sack paper (1.7Mt): Mondi holds 30% to 40%+, Billerud is second, and the top five hold about 40% of specialty kraft. Steep.
  • European FBB (about 4.2Mt to 4.4Mt): the top three hold about 72% (Metsä Board, Stora Enso and Mayr-Melnhof on Jefferies' count; Billerud's 18% appears in another split). Concentrated, but operating rates are in the late 70s.
  • European containerboard (44Mt): the top five hold under 40% (Smurfit Westrock 13%, SAICA 9%, Mondi 6%). Fragmented, with many small, old, non-integrated mills.
  • North America: the top five containerboard producers hold 73% to 74%, and the top five boxboard producers 72%. Consolidated.

Insight for the Consumer: concentration has not protected margins in European board, because the problem is not the number of sellers. It is exit barriers and new entry by conversion (Wisdom Library: "competitive intensity is entry minus exit"). Concentration protects pricing only where capacity is also not growing: LPB, sack and virgin kraftliner, which is where Billerud's price rises are sticking.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

D5846th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares in Billerud's qualified niches (LPB, sack, fluting) have been stable for years. New entrants have taken meaningful share only in recycled containerboard (graphic-paper conversions) and in cartonboard and LPB in Asian export markets (Chinese capacity). Billerud's exposed flank is its Asian LPB and cartonboard exports, not its European core.

The evidence:

  • LPB qualification takes 18 months to several years, and contracts run for multiple years, so shifts are rare. Billerud's 2026 LPB volumes were contracted in line with 2025.
  • But Asian buyers are turning to Chinese and Asian suppliers. Billerud reports increased competition and weaker demand from Southeast Asian LPB buyers. Chinese virgin cartonboard exports doubled to 3.8Mt in 2025. Greatview Aseptic (a converter) grew its European revenue share from 20% to nearly 50% in a decade, which shows entrenched downstream positions can erode.
  • In containerboard, the Norske Skog Golbey (555kt), Heinzel Laakirchen (470kt), Mondi Duino (420kt), Palm King's Lynn (700kt from 2027) and Eren Shotton (750kt) conversions add new recycled supply, though several ran below 50% and EBITDA-negative in 2025 to 2026.
  • In FBB, the new capacity comes from incumbents (Stora Enso Oulu 750kt, Metsä Board Husum +200kt), not entrants.

Over three to five years Billerud's European volumes fell: 2026 volumes of about 2.5Mt are 0.4Mt below 2021 (Jefferies). That was mostly demand and Billerud's own value-over-volume choices, not lost share.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F3813th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest and most advantaged competitors, and is this company among them?

Judgement: in North America, share has consolidated towards the largest players (International Paper with DS Smith, Smurfit Westrock), and closures restored pricing. In Europe, consolidation is slow and has favoured the cost-advantaged rather than the largest: integrated forest owners (SCA, Holmen), producers with low-cost South American pulp (Stora Enso via Veracel and Montes del Plata, Suzano, UPM Uruguay), and integrated converters (Mondi in sack bags). Billerud is not among the most advantaged on cost. It is among the advantaged on product (qualified virgin grades) and, in North America, on fibre and tariffs.

The mechanism matters for the model. In the European virgin grades, the marginal tonne is priced off the costs of non-integrated Nordic mills, of which Billerud is the largest. So when wood is cheap, Billerud's relative position improves and margin gains are real; when wood is dear, integrated peers absorb it and Billerud cannot pass it on. Over a full cycle this is a structural disadvantage: Holmen's ROCE of 12.8% against Billerud's 4.0% in 2025, a gap of more than 8 points.

Consolidation towards the advantaged would require high-cost capacity to exit. Billerud has said it is willing to cut its own capacity. The likely sequence is that Billerud joins closures rather than leads acquisitions.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F4723rd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the Nordic incumbents are mostly margin-first. They schedule downtime rather than cut price (Holmen "value over volume", Metsä Board closed Tako, Stora Enso closed Skutskär line 3, UPM idled Kaukas, Mondi closed converting plants). But three groups are volume-first and willing to suffer losses: Stora Enso while it fills Oulu, Asian exporters with state-aligned financing, and recycled converters ramping new machines. Prices do fall when volume falls in the oversupplied grades (folding boxboard, recycled containerboard, pulp) and hold in the scarce ones.

Evidence:

  • European board producers "have avoided destructive price wars", managing through commercial downtime.
  • A former Stora Enso VP says producers are at "unsustainably low" profit, so customers are unlikely to get further cuts.
  • But folding boxboard prices fell in 2025 to 2026 with operating rates in the late 70s.
  • European cartonboard price rises achieved in H1 are "historically entirely surrendered in H2" (Mayr-Melnhof's experience).
  • SAICA and Prinzhorn's EUR 60 a tonne recycled hike failed in June 2026.
  • Asian producers drive global consumerboard operating rates towards 70%.

For Billerud's mix: benign in sack (Mondi leads up), fluting and kraftliner (Smurfit leads, SCA disciplined). Competitive, not destructive, in LPB. Adverse in cartonboard. This attitude is the main reason the 2026 wood relief is partly retained in scarce grades and partly given back in cartonboard.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4235th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the attitude has been stable in kind (downtime over price war) but has weakened at the margin over 18 to 36 months, because three new pressures arrived together: Oulu's 750kt start-up (2025), Chinese export growth, and tariff-redirected flows into Europe (from August 2025). The most plausible "shark" is Stora Enso, which is behind on Oulu, has cumulative start-up losses of EUR 190m, is highly levered (3.1x) and is preparing a demerger. Metsä Board, which is distressed (leverage 4.5x to 8.6x, no dividend, 18% sales fall in Q1 2026), is the second candidate.

There is no sign of O'Leary-style erratic behaviour from the Nordic incumbents; their behaviour is predictable and cost-driven. But both Stora Enso and Metsä Board have strong incentives to fill machines in 2027:

  • Stora Enso's EBIT margin target (above 10%) and deleveraging need Oulu volume.
  • Metsä Board needs volume to rebuild ROCE from negative.

That makes a price "shark attack" in FBB and possibly LPB in 2027 a real risk. It is not the base case, but it is perhaps a one-in-five chance over 18 months. In kraftliner, the attack would come indirectly from recycled converters (Palm, Eren) pushing down testliner and narrowing the virgin premium. Mondi has been acquisitive (Schumacher EUR 634m) but rational on price. For the model: price give-back risk sits in 2027, concentrated in cartonboard, FBB and LPB renewals, not in sack or fluting.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5030th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: industry structure is supportive for roughly 60% to 65% of Billerud's sales: LPB, sack and specialty kraft, semichemical fluting, virgin kraftliner, and tariff-protected North American graphic and label paper. It is unsupportive for the rest: cartonboard, market pulp, and Asian LPB exports. The support is moderately fragile, because it rests on conditions outside Billerud's control.

Those conditions are:

  • no new fresh-fibre machines;
  • discipline from Stora Enso and Metsä Board while they fill new or idle capacity;
  • the recycled-virgin price gap holding when UK capacity arrives in 2027;
  • US tariffs staying in place;
  • high exit barriers not stopping weaker mills from closing.

The structure is also asymmetric over the cycle. Support shows up as margin in good times, but the structural cost disadvantage (no forest) means Billerud gives up more margin than integrated peers when wood rises. That is why ROCE was 4% in 2025 while Holmen made 12.8%. Over the model horizon: supportive in 2026 to H1 2027, then neutral, with a fragility peak in H2 2027.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?D4426th percentilefull answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D4528th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a mixed and slowly shifting threat. Plastic is losing share to fibre because of regulation, but it is cheaper and is regaining ground where budgets are tight. Recycled board substitutes for virgin wherever strength or hygiene is not critical. Digital media is an irreversible substitute for graphic paper.

  • Plastic. Regulation (PPWR, EPR, bans) is moving demand to fibre, but plastic remains much cheaper. Barrier performance often still needs plastic lamination. Some brands (personal care sticks, pet food) have reverted to plastic for performance. Polymer cost spikes (Hormuz disruptions) temporarily improve fibre's economics.
  • Recycled board. Offers up to 50% cost saving in non-demanding uses (EUR 350 to 400 a tonne against EUR 700 to 800 for premium kraftliner). Virgin fibre is irreplaceable in direct food contact and in the strength-critical uses where Billerud is concentrated (LPB, sack, semichemical fluting).
  • Digital media. Graphic paper is declining 5% to 6.5% a year; European graphic paper fell to 12.8Mt.
  • Lightweighting. Reduces tonnes per pack. Billerud leads it (New Flute), which wins share but shrinks the market.

Changing: the plastic threat is slowly weakening; recycled substitution pressure rises when gas is cheap and new recycled capacity arrives in 2027; the digital threat is constant.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

F3510th percentile

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

F369th percentile8 answers
Judgement36C-4136C-4245C-4335C-4455C-4527C-4638C-4733
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

F369th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Billerud's competitive advantage is real but narrow. It rests on qualified virgin-fibre grades where customers cannot easily switch (LPB, sack, fluting), on energy self-sufficiency, and on a North American fibre-cost and tariff position. It is outweighed through the cycle by its structural lack of forest and its SEK cost base, which leave it as the marginal-cost producer in its European grades. The evidence is in the returns:

  • ROCE of 4.0% in 2025;
  • a European EBIT margin of 3%;
  • a margin target lowered to 15%;
  • a ROCE gap of more than 8 points to Holmen.

Pricing power is demonstrable only in scarce grades and has not survived a cost upcycle. Customer utility is demonstrably not inferior. Mechanical forces are slightly adverse, so margin progress needs continuing management action. For the model:

  • assume Billerud earns its cost of capital only in years when Nordic wood is falling or fresh-fibre prices are rising;
  • do not assume structural margin expansion beyond the identified self-help;
  • assume the competitive position is stable in niches and eroding slowly in cartonboard and LPB exports.
C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

F363rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Billerud holds several real but niche-sized moats and one large structural anti-moat (no forest, with a SEK cost base). Over this cycle the anti-moat has outweighed the moats: ROCE was 4.0% in 2025, below the cost of capital, against Holmen at 12.8%. On Towson's split, most of Billerud's advantages are barriers to entry (qualification, capital intensity), not competitive advantages over existing rivals.

  • Customer-related, strong and stable: qualification lock-in in LPB and food-contact grades. Qualification takes 18 months to several years, contracts are multi-year, and converters' filling lines depend on consistent fibre. This protects volume more than price (contracts lag costs).
  • Production-related.
  • Virgin fibre properties in sack, semichemical fluting and kraftliner (strength): strong, and widening while fresh fibre is scarce.
  • Biomass energy self-sufficiency (Swedish mills about 98% fossil-free): strong, widening during gas shocks, but taxed by carbon rules in 2026.
  • North American fibre cost (hardwood chips at USD 85 against USD 140 a cubic metre in Sweden; local surplus wood): strong and durable.
  • Scale. Number one or two niche positions, enough for minimum efficient scale. Moderate. Being the largest Nordic wood buyer gives no purchasing power.
  • Regulatory or political. US tariffs: moderate and temporary. EUDR certification: small. Carbon: an anti-moat in 2026.
  • Culture and knowledge. A long engineering history, a proven cost-programme capability and a lightweighting innovation record (New Flute): moderate, soft.
  • Anti-moat. Wood self-sufficiency under 5% (about the bottom 5% of European packaging producers), combined with 82% non-SEK revenue against 55% SEK costs. Permanent.

Widening: energy, fresh-fibre scarcity and North American local supply. Narrowing: LPB against Asian producers and Stora Enso, and cartonboard.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

D4531st percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Billerud is mid-sized. That is enough to be efficient in each niche, but not enough to set prices outside sack and fluting, or to win purchasing power over wood. Revenue of SEK 40.5bn (about EUR 3.6bn) compares with:

  • Stora Enso, EUR 9.3bn;
  • Mondi and Smurfit Westrock, much larger;
  • Mayr-Melnhof, EUR 3.9bn;
  • Metsä Board, EUR 1.8bn;
  • Holmen and SCA, smaller in revenue but far more profitable.

Capacity is about 3.1Mt in Europe (six mills), plus 1.1Mt of paper and 0.2Mt of pulp in North America. There are nine production units serving about 2,200 customers in more than 100 countries.

What scale confers:

  • minimum efficient scale in LPB and sack;
  • the ability to fund a SEK 2.4bn to 2.6bn capex programme and run two regions;
  • a portfolio that offsets demand (food and beverage 45% to 52% of sales, defensive);
  • diversification across regions, with North America about 50% of 2025 EBITDA.

What it does not confer: cost leadership (wood is bought at market prices), pricing power in cartonboard or containerboard, or a scale gap over Stora Enso in LPB. Scale here is "a bigger company, not a cheaper one" (Wisdom Library: scale needs a bounded market).

C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?F359th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D5525th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Billerud's customer utility is demonstrably not inferior, and it leads in a few niches. Inferiority is disproved, which is the test at this stage.

Evidence:

  • LPB is qualified with all three major converters (Tetra Pak, SIG, Elopak), which describe Billerud as a "stable supplier" with predictable deliveries.
  • A former Stora Enso VP (21 years' tenure) calls Billerud's assets "well-invested and comparable" to the top Nordic peers.
  • Semichemical fluting is sold out.
  • New Flute delivered up to 25% lighter boxes and 35% lower global-warming potential in customer tests.
  • In North America, local supply gives shorter, more predictable lead times than importers, and Tribute trials "exceeded expectations".
  • European mills are about 98% fossil-free and 96% certified, which matters for brand owners' Scope 3 targets.

Where it is not leading:

  • cartonboard, where Stora Enso's Oulu claims a 30% to 40% yield advantage and Metsä Board leads premium FBB;
  • LPB against Stora Enso's patents and integrated system;
  • sack against Mondi's integrated bag offer.

There is no evidence of a utility gap that would cost share.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F2724th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Billerud's demonstrable pricing power is limited to its scarce and qualified niches, and to North America behind tariffs. Across the group it is weak, and the decisive evidence is historical: when Nordic wood doubled between 2021 and 2025, Billerud could not pass it on. Region Europe's EBIT margin fell to 3% in 2025, and the group EBITDA margin target had to be lowered from 17% to 15%.

Evidence of applied price:

  • LPB +8% to 9% at the start of 2024, through contract renegotiation with escalation and indexation clauses added. A 5% to 10% LPB price move is worth SEK 0.4bn to 0.8bn.
  • Sack +EUR 50 a tonne into Q4 2026 (industry, Mondi-led).
  • European fluting and containerboard increases in Q2 and Q3 2026, which pushed Europe's margin to 12%.
  • North American graphic and label price rises in Q2 2026, and North America sustaining 16% to 22% margins.
  • Logistics surcharges.

Evidence against:

  • Year-on-year selling prices were lower in Q2 and Q3 2026.
  • Producers "struggled to raise prices enough to offset structurally high Nordic wood costs" (Jefferies).
  • Cartonboard gives back cost relief.
  • LPB contracts reprice with a lag and against inflation indices, not wood costs.

The Wisdom Library's "pricing power" test (does a price rise stick once its cost reverses?) has not been passed: today's rises are mostly cost-driven. For the model: treat price as a 2026 to H1 2027 lever, not a structural one.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

D3843rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: not in the Costco or Ryanair sense. Billerud's strategy is explicitly "profitability over volume" and "value over volume". That is the opposite of sharing scale economies through lower prices: it accepts lower volume to protect price. Billerud does share value through product performance:

  • lightweighting (New Flute's up to 25% weight reduction, and up to 10% supply-chain cost savings it cites for customers);
  • co-development (BoxLab testing);
  • predictable local supply in North America.

This is value-based selling, not scale-economy sharing. That is appropriate for a non-cost-leader, but it means Billerud does not build the customer loyalty that comes from consistently being the cheapest. In oversupplied grades customers simply take cost savings back. This is neither a strength nor a weakness for the case. It confirms that Billerud's economics depend on spread and product, not scale.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F3313th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the mechanical forces are slightly negative overall. Improvement depends largely on management action, which the Business Foundations report calls "heroic" cost discipline against "severe mechanical headwinds".

Mechanical forces working for Billerud:

  • the cumulative cost programmes (each saved krona stays unless inflation erodes it);
  • North America's persistent fibre-cost advantage (local growth of 12.6 million against removals of 5.1 million cubic metres);
  • the North American mix shift towards packaging as Evolution volumes ramp;
  • biomass energy against rising carbon and gas costs for rivals;
  • falling capex after 2027 (towards a SEK 2.2bn base), which raises free cash flow.

Mechanical forces working against:

  • graphic-paper decline of about 5% a year on about SEK 9bn of sales, which removes about SEK 0.45bn of revenue a year;
  • the structural Nordic wood deficit (the Russian ban) and tightening forestry rules, against peers that own forest and gain from the same price;
  • the carbon cost path (ETS2 from 2028);
  • wage and maintenance inflation on an ageing North American asset base;
  • possibly the Gävle boiler, a large reinvestment just to stand still.

Against SCA and Holmen, time does not favour Billerud: their forest values compound while Billerud's wood bill rises. Against Mondi and Metsä Board, time is roughly neutral. The model should not assume margin improvement without named actions.

4b

Management quality

D5237th percentile12 answers
Judgement52C-5537C-5650C-5755C-57b52C-5845C-5958C-6066C-6160C-6240C-6352C-6440
Group judgement

Company - Management

D5237th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Billerud's management is a competent, disciplined, finance-led operator team. It makes good capital-protection decisions (cancelling Escanaba, exiting Viken, cutting capex), delivers cost programmes early, communicates the near-term transmission mechanism unusually well, and has strengthened the regional bench with external industry operators. It is not an outlier team:

  • ambition is defensive;
  • strategy relies on external events to reach its European target;
  • the record against market expectations is volatile;
  • standards on safety and North American execution are below par;
  • capital allocation favours resilience over returns (dividend cut, no buybacks at book-value lows).

Governance is prudent and clean. For the Consumer, management quality supports high confidence in the controllable building blocks of the forecast: savings, capex, maintenance planning and leverage. It does not justify assuming that management can offset adverse wood, price or currency moves beyond those blocks. The departure of the EVP Legal & Strategic Projects and the newness of the regional presidents are the watch-points for 2027 execution.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

F377th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Billerud's ambition is moderate and defensive. It aims to be the best-run non-integrated virgin-fibre producer ("profitability over volume", "value over volume", "put profitability over sales growth"), not to grow or to reshape its industry.

The headline ambition is an absolute EBITDA above SEK 6bn over a cycle, with a margin above 15% and ROCE above 11%. In absolute terms that is ambitious: it is about 50% above 2025 and above every year except 2022. In method it is conservative: cost, discipline, incremental North American conversion, and willingness to cut capacity. The vision statement ("high performance packaging materials for a low carbon society") is aligned with plastic substitution, but there is no investment programme behind it in Europe.

Against peers:

  • Stora Enso is more ambitious and more transformational (Oulu, the forest demerger, a target above 10% EBIT margin).
  • Mondi is more acquisitive (Schumacher).
  • Metsä Board has a clear growth target ("Lead the Pack": Consumer Packaging growth above 4% a year).
  • Mayr-Melnhof is broadly similar (self-help).

Billerud sits at the defensive end. That fits its structural position (no forest and a SEK cost base make growth capex unattractive), but it means there is no growth optionality to value beyond North American packaging and possible consolidation.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F5015th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: the strategy is smart and right for the company's position, but not bold. It protects capital and widens the moat only at the edges.

What is smart:

  1. Cancelling the SEK 7bn Escanaba conversion before cost inflation and a weak market destroyed value. Peers who pressed on (Stora Enso Oulu, with EUR 190m of cumulative start-up losses; Norske Skog Golbey, with capex escalating to EUR 320m and negative EBITDA) show what was avoided.
  2. Converting North America incrementally (SEK 1.3bn), using low-cost local fibre and tariff protection, while running graphic paper for cash.
  3. Focusing Europe on mix and on high-performance fresh-fibre niches, and openly accepting capacity cuts.
  4. Two cost programmes, run in sequence.
  5. Explicit regional targets.

What is missing:

  • a lever on the structural wood disadvantage, such as long-term wood partnerships at scale or securing forest access (the Viken BCTMP import project was exited);
  • a European growth engine;
  • a decisive footprint move.

The strategy can capitalise on a range of environments: in a strong market it keeps price and volume discipline, and in a weak market it has optionality to close. But it relies on external events (closures, wood) to reach its own European target. Jefferies calls the European target unrealistic.

C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

D5530th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Ivar Vatne is a capable, disciplined operator-manager and an enabler, not an ossifier. He is not an "animal" chief executive. His actions show good capital judgement and cost delivery. His record on earnings delivery against the market is mixed, and his growth record is untested.

The actions:

  • August 2023 (acting chief executive): reorganised into two regions within weeks.
  • May 2024: cancelled the SEK 7bn conversion (a decision of the board and management).
  • December 2024: set the Way Forward strategy and targets.
  • 2023 to 2024: closed the SEK 1.5bn programme a year early, though it was launched by his predecessor, Christoph Michalski.
  • September 2025: launched the SEK 800m programme and executed all 650 position reductions by Q1 2026.
  • Q1 2026: exited the Viken joint venture.
  • Hiring: replaced both regional presidents with external industry operators.
  • Capex: cut guidance twice.

Against him:

  • profit misses (Q2 2025 by 28% and Q1 2026 by 24% at EBITDA level);
  • the 2025 dividend cut, which surprised the market;
  • North American shutdown overruns;
  • no open-market share purchases at the 2026 trough (he holds 51,658 shares, worth about SEK 4.6m);
  • an almost entirely finance background (Procter & Gamble, Fiskars, Arla Foods UK CFO), with no prior P&L leadership of a mill business.

Experts and brokers see a credible operator who "has yet to convince the market of long-term structural value". He is 48, has been at Billerud since 2019, and carries no visible tenure risk.

D5246th percentile

Judgement: hiring is a visible priority, and the external hires are strong and relevant. The team as a whole is thinner and more recently formed than it looks, with turnover in key positions.

The team looks capable of execution. It is not yet proven as a group through a full cycle.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

F4516th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the business is adequately but not generously resourced. The European mills are well-invested and comparable to top Nordic peers (a former Stora Enso VP). The North American assets are older and need maintenance and upgrade capex. Billerud has also been spending below depreciation, which is cash-positive now but stores up a reinvestment need (Gävle).

  • Capex. SEK 2.4bn (2026), SEK 2.6bn (2027, including SEK 0.5bn strategic), then a base of about SEK 2.2bn, against depreciation of about SEK 2.87bn. Capex intensity is about 6% of sales, against 7% to 10% at Mondi and Stora Enso historically.
  • Workforce. Cut by 15% over three years. Employee stress is elevated and turnover is rising (8.7%). Safety (LTIFR 5.5) is weak.
  • Balance sheet. A buffer at 2.0x, with no refinancing strain visible.
  • Invested for resilience. Biomass energy (98% fossil-free in Europe), certified wood (over 96%), the Sveaskog and Bergvik Skog Öst wood agreements to 2034, a captive insurer, ERP harmonisation, the woodyard at Escanaba (on time and on budget), the PM6 headbox, and the PM8 Yankee cylinder.
  • Not invested. Forest ownership; pulp import alternatives (the Viken exit); the Gävle boiler (deferred to a 2027 decision).

Billerud is "built yesterday for resilience" on energy and certification, and less so on fibre and people.

C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

B+6689th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: communication is clear, plain-spoken and unusually helpful on the near-term transmission mechanism, but it stops at the quarter and the strategic narrative. It gives no annual financial guidance and no bridge to the SEK 6bn target.

Helpful disclosures:

  • quarterly savings delivered (SEK 100m, 150m and 150m);
  • maintenance shutdown schedules, with cost per mill and budget;
  • input-cost guidance in kronor (SEK 60m for Q4 2026, two-thirds logistics);

  • the dividend cut came as a surprise;
  • a gap between "solid North American conditions" and "Evolution progress" and what US converters say (SBS oversupply; "wild card").

For a modeller, Billerud gives most of the building blocks needed quarter by quarter, but not the bridge.

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

C6059th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking?

  • the cost programmes were delivered early;
  • the SEK 7bn conversion was cancelled;
  • the joint venture was exited;

C-62

To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

F4022nd percentile

Judgement: Billerud does not hold outlier standards. It is a solid, above-average executor on cost speed and a below-average one on margins, safety and North American operations.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

C5259th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: governance is prudent and clean, and decisions have mostly protected shareholders' capital. Shareholder returns, however, have been cut, and management's personal alignment is modest.

Evidence of prudence:

  • All seven board members are independent of management, and six of seven are independent of major owners.
  • There are no dual-class shares.
  • The board approves eight top-level policies annually and runs enterprise risk management.
  • There is a Speak-Up line reporting quarterly to the audit committee.
  • There have been no material weaknesses or restatements in three years.
  • Related-party dealings are disclosed (SEK 551m with the BSÖ group; SEK 352m with Bomhus Energi).
  • Hedging requires board approval beyond 15 months and 80%.
  • Net debt is capped at 2.5x EBITDA.
  • KPMG has been auditor since 2015, a long tenure but routinely re-elected.

Evidence of shareholders first:

  • The SEK 7bn conversion was cancelled.
  • Capex was cut.
  • Short-term pay is tied to ROCE and operating cash flow, and long-term pay to TSR, with participants required to invest in shares.

Against:

  • The dividend was cut to SEK 2.00 (prudent for the balance sheet, but a reduction).
  • There have been no buybacks.
  • Group management pay was SEK 66m in total.
  • US executives' variable pay is capped at 150% of salary.
  • The chief executive's holding is modest relative to pay, and the CFO holds 9,780 shares.
  • No insider bought shares in the open market at the trough.

Frapag's board seat (12.1%) gives a large owner a direct voice, which supports alignment.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

D4030th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: Billerud is a careful capital allocator, but not an enlightened or dynamic one. Its record includes one good large deal, one good large cancellation, high-return cost programmes and no buybacks. Its group ROCE has been below the cost of capital for four years.

The positives:

  • Verso (2022): bought cheaply. North America produced about 50% of group EBITDA in 2025, and the deal created the tariff-protected platform.
  • Escanaba: a SEK 7bn commitment was avoided when returns turned unfavourable.
  • Self-help: high-return cost programmes.
  • Viken: a capital commitment was avoided.

The negatives:

  • Capex of SEK 3.2bn to 3.3bn in 2022 to 2023 into a downturn.
  • Dividends partly debt-funded in 2023 and 2025 (SEK 1.9bn paid in 2023 on reported net income of SEK 0.48bn).
  • The SEK 3.5bn rights issue for Verso diluted shareholders.
  • A SEK 189m write-off on the Escanaba study.
  • Gävle (above SEK 3bn) is a forthcoming low-return replacement investment.
  • No share repurchases despite trading at 0.55x to 0.6x book for much of 2026.

The repurchase gap is telling. At an all-time-low price to book, an enlightened allocator with leverage of 2x would at least have discussed buybacks. Management chose resilience. For the model: no buybacks before 2028, a dividend at 50% to 60% of earnings, and a capex step-up if Gävle is approved.

Financial profile

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F266th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Billerud's economic strength is weak through the cycle and improving from a trough. The record shows:

  • returns on capital below the cost of capital (ROCE 4.0% in 2025; about 6% on FY2027 consensus; a target of above 11% that needs about SEK 3.7bn of EBIT);
  • EPS that swings with the spread and has not compounded since Verso;
  • thin and erratic free cash flow (negative in FY2023 and FY2025 on FactSet's definition);
  • an EBIT margin structurally lowered by depreciation of about 7% of sales.

Against this:

  • the balance sheet is comfortable (2.0x, falling);
  • the earnings base is reasonably clean at EBITDA level, once carbon allowance income is removed;
  • self-help has delivered SEK 2.3bn of savings.

The defining feature for the Consumer is operating leverage: about SEK 0.4bn of EBITDA per 1% of price, about SEK 0.9bn per 10% of wood, about SEK 0.64bn per 10% of krona, and SEK 0.31 of EPS per SEK 100m of EBITDA. Those make the FY2027 EPS cone roughly SEK 2.3 to 6.7 around a central SEK 4.1 to 4.8. Mid-term potential in a neutral cycle is about SEK 5.0bn to 5.5bn of EBITDA, which supports EPS of about SEK 5.5 to 7 by 2028 to 2029, not SEK 9. Capital deployment opportunities are limited and partly defensive (Gävle), which argues for a cyclical, book-value-anchored multiple rather than a growth multiple.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F1811th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Billerud generates no durable EPS growth. EPS is a cyclical spread outcome that swings by a factor of three to eight between troughs and peaks, on revenue that has been flat apart from the 2022 Verso acquisition. The near-term growth (consensus +328% in FY2027) is a mechanical recovery from a trough, not compounding.

History:

  • Reported basic EPS: 4.60 (FY2019), 3.04, 6.69, 20.13 (FY2022 peak), 1.95, 7.03, 2.86 (FY2025).
  • Brokers' basis: 2.45 (FY2023), 6.24 (FY2024), 2.88 (FY2025).
  • Revenue: about SEK 24bn to 26bn in FY2019 to FY2021, then SEK 42.7bn, 41.6bn, 43.4bn and 40.5bn after Verso.
  • Organic revenue growth: -7% (2023), +6% (2024), -3% (2025).
  • Shares: 222m to 249m, after the 2022 issue that funded Verso. No buybacks since.

Consensus: EPS 1.32 (FY2026), 5.63 (FY2027), 6.51 (FY2028), 9.10 (FY2029, one broker), on revenue growth of -1.5%, +4.2%, +3.2% and +4.5%.

My judgement for the model: FY2027 EPS of SEK 4.1 to 4.8 centrally, FY2028 of about SEK 5.0 to 5.8. This is below consensus because I expect part of 2026's cost relief to be offset by currency, wood normalisation and price give-back.

Durability is low for two reasons. Operating leverage: each SEK 100m of EBITDA is SEK 0.31 of EPS. And the drivers (wood, price, krona) mean-revert. The best guide to "normal" EPS is the FY2024 level of about SEK 6.2, reached with wood near its peak but with surplus carbon allowance income and a stronger dollar. On average, through the cycle, EPS has been around SEK 5 to 6 since Verso.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F242nd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is little scope to deploy capital at a high return. The fair multiple is therefore a cyclical, asset-based one, not a growth multiple.

The available projects:

  1. US Evolution: SEK 1.3bn, of which about SEK 460m remains for 2027. The target is 300kt of North American packaging by 2030. Returns are unproven, since pricing in North American SBS and liner is contested, but the capital is modest. If 300kt displaced graphic tonnes at a margin of, say, SEK 1,000 to 2,000 a tonne better, the return would be adequate. That is plausible, not demonstrated.
  2. The Gävle recovery boiler: above SEK 3bn, decision in 2027. This is replacement and energy efficiency: it reduces energy use and fossil emissions, which matters under ETS2. The return is defensive and low, roughly the cost of capital.
  3. Self-help. SEK 0.8bn of savings for about SEK 350m of restructuring charges, and OEE gains for little capex. These are the only clearly high-return uses, and they are largely used up.
  4. No European growth capex is wanted, because of oversupply, and M&A is only mooted.

Implication: mid-term EBITDA potential is about SEK 5.0bn to 5.5bn in a neutral cycle, not SEK 6bn. With ROCE below the cost of capital, the stock should trade on EV/EBITDA of about 5.5x to 7x mid-cycle EBITDA, or about 0.6x to 1.0x book, rather than on P/E growth. At SEK 89 it trades at EV/EBITDA of about 5.7x FY2027 consensus (6.0x on my central case), and about 0.8x consensus FY2026 book (SEK 111 a share). That is fair for a mid-cycle recovery that is still unproven.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

F174th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: returns are low and below the cost of capital through this cycle. The target (ROCE above 11%) needs EBIT of about SEK 3.7bn, which is about 75% above FY2027 consensus EBIT of SEK 2.1bn, and has not been reached since 2022.

  • ROCE. 4.0% in 2025 (company). The 2026 estimate is about 2%. The 2027 estimate is about 6% on consensus EBIT of SEK 2,103m and capital employed of about SEK 33bn to 34bn (equity of about SEK 27.6bn plus net debt of about SEK 6.0bn including leases). Post-tax, that is about 4.5% to 5%, against an estimated cost of capital of 8% to 9%.
  • Tangible capital. Capital employed is almost entirely tangible: tangible assets of SEK 29.5bn, with little goodwill. Verso was bought cheaply. So return on tangible capital equals ROCE, and is just as low.
  • Return on new invested capital. Very high on the cost programmes (SEK 2.3bn of savings since 2023 for a few hundred million of one-off cost, before inflation erodes them). Unproven on Evolution. Negative on the abandoned Escanaba study (a SEK 189m write-off in 2024) and the Viken joint venture (SEK 47m to 50m).
  • Peers. Holmen 12.8%, Mayr-Melnhof 5.3% (adjusted, 2025), Stora Enso 3.7% (Q1 2026), Metsä Board negative.

Billerud is mid-pack among the non-integrated producers and far below the integrated ones. Depreciation of about SEK 2.87bn a year exceeds capex of SEK 2.4bn to 2.6bn. This supports cash flow, but signals that reported EBIT understates economic returns less than it might seem: assets are being consumed faster than they are replaced, except where Gävle forces catch-up spending.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F186th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: free cash flow conversion is poor and erratic in the reported history and only adequate in consensus. Capex absorbs most of operating cash flow, and the recent strong quarterly cash conversion is helped by working capital that cannot be released again.

  • FactSet free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex): SEK 2,621m (FY2022), -1,734m (FY2023), 164m (FY2024), -198m (FY2025). As a share of net income: 57%, -358%, 9% and -28%. Operating cash flow is a steady SEK 3.0bn to 3.3bn; capex is SEK 2.4bn to 3.3bn; dividends were SEK 0.5bn to 1.9bn. So dividends were partly debt-financed in 2023 and 2025.
  • Company cash conversion (operating cash flow over adjusted EBITDA, a different measure): 77% in 2025, 55% in Q1 2026, 97% in Q2 and 127% in Q3. The target is above 80% over a cycle.
  • Consensus free cash flow: SEK 681m (FY2026), 1,432m (FY2027), 1,420m (FY2028), about 2.0x, 1.0x and 0.9x net income.
  • Working capital. About 13% of trailing revenue on FactSet's definition (SEK 5.1bn at Q3 2026), or 9.6% of sales in 2025 on the company's. Receivables fell from SEK 4.76bn to 3.71bn in 2025 (SEK 677m released). Supplier finance terms of up to 127 days hold payables up. This is a once-only gain; it reverses as volumes and prices rise.

My judgement for the model: structural free cash flow at EBITDA of SEK 4.6bn is about SEK 1.0bn to 1.3bn. That is EBITDA, less capex of about SEK 2.6bn, interest of about 0.3bn, tax of about 0.3bn to 0.4bn, leases and pensions of about 0.1bn to 0.2bn, and a working-capital build of about 0.1bn to 0.3bn as prices rise. It is about SEK 4 to 5 a share, a 4.5% to 6% yield. Gävle would cut it by about SEK 1bn a year while being built.

C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

F207th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: margins are at a cyclical trough and turning up, but structurally lower than before 2022 because of the Nordic wood shock. The FY2027 to FY2028 consensus margins equal Billerud's long-run average, not its target.

  • Gross margin. FactSet's gross line (2.6% in FY2025) is effectively EBIT after depreciation and is not meaningful. Ignore it.
  • EBITDA margin, brokers' basis. 12.3% (FY2024), 10.1% (FY2025), 8.7% (FY2026E), 11.8% (FY2027E), 12.3% (FY2028E), 13.6% (FY2029E, one broker).

Against peers on consensus FY2027 EBITDA margin: Billerud 12%, Mayr-Melnhof 12%, Mondi 12%, Metsä Board 12%, Stora Enso 15%. On EBIT margin, Billerud is lowest, at 5% against 6% to 9%, because of its heavy depreciation.

Trend: up from the 2026 trough. The consensus FY2027 margin of 11.8% requires the European margin to hold at the Q3 2026 level (about 12%) through a seasonally weaker Q4 and Q1, and North America to return to about 18%. My central margin is about 11% to 11.5%.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

C-4255th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the earnings base is moderately clean at the EBITDA level and noisy below it. The Consumer must be careful about four items:

  1. Carbon allowance income sat inside adjusted EBITDA. Net sales of surplus allowances were about SEK 507m in FY2025 and SEK 120m in Q2 2025. This flattered the FY2024 to FY2025 base. Its absence is a large part of the FY2026 trough. FY2027 consensus should not assume it returns.

  2. Items affecting comparability are frequent: - restructuring of SEK 350m (Q3 2025); - the Viken write-off of about SEK 47m to 50m (Q1 2026); - a US pension settlement gain of SEK 389m and US transformation costs of SEK 278m (2024); - a landfill divestment gain of SEK 70m (2024); - an Escanaba write-off of SEK 189m (2024); - an inventory revaluation of SEK 310m (Q2 2023). They are roughly balanced over time, but they make reported quarters unreliable (operating loss of SEK -58m in Q2 2026 against adjusted EBITDA of SEK 661m).

  3. The EPS basis spread is very large. FY2025 EPS was 2.86 reported, 2.88 on the brokers' basis, 4.65 on FactSet's adjusted diluted basis and 5.42 on the Viewforth definition. Use the brokers' basis for comparison with consensus and model reported tax.

  4. The tax rate is volatile on a reported basis (58.1% in FY2025, -55.1% in FY2023), but normal is about 22% to 22.6%. There is no structural leakage.

Other points:

  • There are no capitalised development costs of note.
  • Supplier finance supports cash, not earnings.
  • Hedging results run through revenue (SEK +45m in H1 2026, SEK -18m in Q2).
  • There are no minorities or associates of significance.
  • The Risks report's "false friend" claim, that pension gains hide Swedish losses, refers to a 2024 one-off and is not a live distortion.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D6248th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: financial leverage is moderate and comfortable. It amplifies EPS modestly, through about SEK 0.3bn of annual interest (about SEK 0.95 a share before tax), and it is the strongest balance sheet among the non-integrated European peers.

  • Net debt. About SEK 5.1bn on the company definition (excluding leases). FactSet shows SEK 6.0bn at Q3 2026, including leases and other debt. Pension provisions are about SEK 0.5bn and right-of-use assets about SEK 0.3bn.
  • Net debt to EBITDA. 2.0x at Q3 2026 (company), down from 2.2x at June. FactSet's trailing figure is 2.31x. The ceiling is 2.5x.

  • Peers. Stora Enso 3.1x (Q1 2026), Mondi 3.2x to 3.3x, Metsä Board 4.5x to 8.6x, Mayr-Melnhof 1.8x to 2.2x.

Risk: a repeat of the 2026 trough in 2027 would keep leverage near 2x but not breach covenants or the ceiling. Gävle (above SEK 3bn) would hold leverage near 1.5x to 2.0x through 2028 to 2029. The balance sheet is not a source of equity risk. It is also not being used to enhance returns.

Pricing power
